\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\bigg(\Theta(1)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}+o\big(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}\pm O(\rho nb\alpha^{2})\bigg)\bigg].

Using the fact that
\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}<0
for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}
, we further have

\displaystyle(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\bigg(\Theta(1)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}+O(\rho nb\alpha^{2})\bigg)\bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle=(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\bigg(\Theta(1)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}+O(\rho nb\alpha^{2})\bigg)\bigg],

where the second equality holds since
(1+o(1))^{2}=1+o(1)
. Further take a summation over
r\in[m]
leads to

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\leq\bigg[\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\bigg]\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\bigg(\Theta(1)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}+O(\rho nb\alpha^{2})\bigg)\bigg].

(A.10)

Similarly, we can also get that

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}\leq\bigg[\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}\bigg]\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\bigg(\Theta(1)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}+O(\rho nb\alpha^{2})\bigg)\bigg].

(A.11)

Regarding the loss derivative
\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}
, we can get that for any
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=1-\mathrm{Logit}_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})=\frac{\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]}{1+\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]}\leq\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]

(A.12)

Before moving to the analysis on the feature, we first show that the model weight corresponding to the wrong label will not learn the noise of the data, i.e.,
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|

will be very small for all
q\in[P]
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
. Particularly, we have the following by (
A.2
)

\displaystyle\max_{r,s}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{r,s}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\ell_{2,s}^{(t)}\cdot\widetilde{\Theta}(d\sigma_{p}^{2})+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\neq s||p\neq q}|\ell_{2,s}^{(t)}|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\max_{r,s}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(nPd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)\bigg],

where the second inequality is due to
|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\leq 1
and
\ell_{2,s}^{(t)}<0
for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
. Therefore, we can get that for all
t\in\big[T_{0},T_{1}^{\prime}\big]
, where
T_{1}^{\prime}\leq\widetilde{O}\big(1/(\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2})\big)
, that

\displaystyle\max_{r,s}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{r,s}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{0})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\bigg[1+\widetilde{O}\big(\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)\bigg]^{\widetilde{O}\big(\frac{1}{\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\max_{r,s}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{0})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}\sigma_{0}),

(A.13)

where the last equality is by Lemma
A.2
. Therefore, we can get the following bound on
F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})
for any
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
,

\displaystyle F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p=1}^{P}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}-\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p=1}^{P}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}+\alpha^{2}\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{u})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\bm{\xi})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}-\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}.

Then by Lemma
A.4
and (
A.2
), we can further get that

\displaystyle F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\leq O(b\alpha^{2})\cdot\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}-\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)+\widetilde{O}\big(mPd\sigma_{p}^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}\big),

where we use the fact that
|\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{u})|\leq b
and
(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle/\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}=o(\alpha^{2})
by Lemma
A.4
.
This further implies the following according to (
A.12
): for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\bigg[O(b\alpha^{2})\cdot\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}-\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)\bigg],

where we use the fact that
mPd\sigma_{p}^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}=o(1)
. Similarly, we can also get that for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}
,

\displaystyle\ell_{2,i}^{(t)}\leq 2\exp\bigg[O(b\alpha^{2})\cdot\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}-\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)\bigg].

Consequently, let
a_{t}:=\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}
and
b_{t}:=\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}
,
further applying (
A.10
) and (
A.11
) gives

\displaystyle a_{t+1}

\displaystyle\leq a_{t}\cdot\Big[1+\Theta(\eta)\cdot\exp\big[O(b\alpha^{2})\cdot b_{t}-a_{t}\big]+O(\eta\rho b\alpha^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle b_{t+1}

\displaystyle\leq b_{t}\cdot\Big[1+\Theta(\eta)\cdot\exp\big[O(b\alpha^{2})\cdot a_{t}-b_{t}\big]+O(\eta\rho b\alpha^{2})\Big].

Then we will first prove a weaker argument on
a_{t}
and
b_{t}
: for all
t\leq T_{1}^{\prime}
it holds that
a_{t},b_{t}=o\big(1/(b\alpha^{2})\big)
. In particular, we will apply standard induction techniques. First, it is easy to verify that this condition holds for
t=T_{0}
according to Lemma
A.3
. Then assuming this condition holds for all
\tau\leq t
, we have
\exp\big[O(b\alpha^{2})\cdot b_{t}\big],\exp\big[O(b\alpha^{2})\cdot a_{t}\big]=\Theta(1)
and thus

\displaystyle a_{\tau+1}

\displaystyle\leq a_{\tau}\cdot\Big[1+\Theta(\eta)\cdot\exp(-a_{\tau})+O(\eta\rho b\alpha^{2})\Big],

\displaystyle b_{\tau+1}

\displaystyle\leq b_{\tau}\cdot\Big[1+\Theta(\eta)\cdot\exp(-b_{\tau})+O(\eta\rho b\alpha^{2})\Big],

(A.14)

for all
\tau\in[T_{0},t]
.
Then by Lemma
A.6
, we can immediately get that

\displaystyle a_{t+1}\leq O\bigg(\log\bigg(\frac{1}{\rho b\alpha^{2}}\cdot e^{t\eta\rho b\alpha^{2}}\bigg)\bigg).

Then recall that
T_{1}^{\prime}=O\big(1/(\eta\rho b\alpha^{2})\big)
and
t\leq T_{1}^{\prime}
, we can further get
a_{t+1},b_{t+1}=O\big(\log(\frac{1}{\rho b\alpha^{2}})\big)
, which verify the hypothesis that
a_{t+1},b_{t+1}\leq o(1/(b\alpha^{2}))
. Moreover, recall the definitions of
a_{t}
and
b_{t}
:
a_{t}:=\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}
and
b_{t}:=\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}
, we can further get that for all
r\in[m]
,

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}\bigg(\log^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{1}{\rho b\alpha^{2}}\bigg)\bigg)=O\big(\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big),

and
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle|=O\big(\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)
. This completes the proof.

∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\{a_{t}\}_{t=0,\dots,}
be a sequence with
a_{0}\in[0,1]
that satisfies

\displaystyle a_{t+1}=a_{t}\cdot[1+c_{1}\cdot e^{-a_{t}}+c_{2}],

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are two constants satisfying
c_{1},c_{2}\in[0,1]
and
c_{2}\leq c_{1}
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle a_{t}\leq O\Big(\log(c_{1}/c_{2})\cdot e^{2c_{2}t}\Big).

Proof of Lemma
A.6
.

Note that
c_{2}\leq c_{1}
, we will then consider two cases: (1)
c_{1}e^{-a_{t}}\geq c_{2}
and (2)
c_{1}e^{-a_{t}}<c_{2}
. Then case (2) will occur after case (1) since
a_{t}
is strictly increasing. Regarding case (1), it is easy to see that
a_{t}\leq\log(c_{1}/c_{2})
by the condition that
c_{1}e^{-a_{t}}\geq c_{2}
. For case (2),
let
t_{0}
be the first iteration
t
that
c_{1}e^{-a_{t}}<c_{2}
, we can get that
a_{t_{0}}=O\big(\log(c_{1}/c_{2})\big)
and then for all
t>t_{0}

\displaystyle a_{t+1}\leq a_{t}\cdot[1+2c_{2}],

which implies that

\displaystyle a_{t}\leq a_{t_{0}}\cdot[1+2c_{2}]^{t-T_{0}}\leq O\Big(\log(c_{1}/c_{2})\cdot e^{2c_{2}t}\Big).

Combining the results for case (1) and case (2), we can complete the proof.

∎

Then we will focus on the rare feature data. Note that in the early stage of the second phase, their corresponding loss derivatives
\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}
’s are still in the constant order. The following Lemma summarizes the learning of rare features and noises for the rare feature data.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
T_{1}=O\big(\frac{n\log(1/(\sigma_{0}d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}))}{d\sigma^{2}\eta}\big)
be a quantity that satisfies
T_{0}<T_{1}<T_{1}^{\prime}
. Then for any
t\in[T_{0},T_{1}]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle\cdot\big[1+\Theta(\rho\eta)\big],\ \langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle\cdot\big[1-\Theta(\rho\eta)\big]

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle\cdot\big[1-\Theta(\rho\eta)\big],\ \langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle\cdot\big[1+\Theta(\rho\eta)\big]

Besides, for any
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
and
k=y_{s}
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{r,p}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|=\max_{r,p}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\widetilde{\Theta}(d\sigma_{p}^{2})\bigg];

for
k\neq y_{s}
,

\displaystyle\max_{r,p}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(d^{-1/2}n).

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
A.2
, except the proof for the dynamics of
\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle
.
First, by standard concentration argument, we can get with probability
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, for all
\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}
, it holds that

\displaystyle d\sigma_{p}^{2}-\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)\leq\|\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq d\sigma_{p}^{2}+\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big).

Then by (
A.2
), we can get

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\ell_{k,s}^{(t)}\cdot\|\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg]\pm\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\neq s||p\neq q}|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\ell_{k,s}^{(t)}\cdot d\sigma_{p}^{2}\bigg]\pm\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big).

Then let
\zeta=O\big(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)
be some user-defined constant, then let
T^{\prime}
be the smallest iteration number such that
\max_{i}|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\in[0.5-\zeta,0.5+\zeta]
. Then we can get for any
t\leq T^{\prime}
and any
i,r
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot(0.5\pm\zeta)\cdot d\sigma_{p}^{2}\bigg]\pm\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big).

(A.15)

Then we will prove the main arguments via mathematical induction, including the following hypothesis:

•

For all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
, it holds that
\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|\geq\frac{1}{n^{0.1}}\cdot\max_{r,i^{\prime},q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(q)}\rangle|

•

\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|=\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot(0.5\pm 2\zeta)\cdot d\sigma_{p}^{2}\bigg]
.

Then it is clear that the first argument holds for
t=T_{0}
as with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
we have
\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{0})},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|=\widetilde{\Theta}(\sigma_{0}d^{1/2}\sigma_{p})
and
\max_{r,i^{\prime},q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(q)}\rangle|=\widetilde{\Theta}(\sigma_{0}d^{1/2}\sigma_{p})
, which implies that
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|\geq\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\cdot\max_{r,i^{\prime},q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(q)}\rangle|
.

Besides, given the first argument, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{O}(nPd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2})\cdot\max_{r,i^{\prime},q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(q)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq O(n^{1.1}Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2})\cdot\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\zeta\cdot d\sigma_{p}^{2}\cdot\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|,

where we use the fact that
d^{-1/2}n^{1.1}P=o\big(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)=o(\zeta)
. Then by (
A.15
), we can directly obtain the second argument.

Now we will verify the hypotheses by induction. First, similar to the previous derivation, the first argument at the
t
-th iteration can directly imply the second argument at the
t+1
-th iteration. Then it remains to verify the first argument. In fact, given the second argument, we have for any
i
and
i^{\prime}
and
\tau\leq t
,

\displaystyle\frac{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|}{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(p)}\rangle|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot(0.5+2\zeta)\cdot d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot(0.5-2\zeta)\cdot d\sigma_{p}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|}{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(p)}\rangle|}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(1+\frac{\eta\zeta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)^{T_{1}}\cdot\frac{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|}{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(p)}\rangle|}.

Therefore, using the fact that
T_{1}=O\big(\frac{n\log(1/(\sigma_{0}d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}))}{d\sigma^{2}\eta}\big)
, setting
\zeta=1/\log^{2}(1/(\sigma_{0}d^{1/2}\sigma_{p})
, we can directly get that

\displaystyle\frac{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|}{\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(p)}\rangle|}\leq\bigg(1+\frac{\eta\zeta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)^{T_{1}}\cdot O(\mathrm{polylog}(n))=o(n^{0.1}).

Note that the above holds for all
i
and
i^{\prime}
, taking
i^{\prime}=\arg\max_{i}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i^{\prime}}^{(p)}\rangle|
directly completes the verification of the first argument.

The proof for
\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|
with
k\neq y_{s}
, we have the following by (
A.2
),

\displaystyle\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\neq s||p\neq q}|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{0})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+T_{1}P\eta\cdot\widetilde{O}(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{O}(d^{-1/2}n),

where we use the fact that for all
t\leq T_{1}
, it holds that
\max_{i,r,q}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(1)
.

∎

Lemma A.8

(End of Phase 2)
.

Let
T_{1}
be the same quantity defined in Lemma
A.7
, we have for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}
,

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|,|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}\rangle|=\widetilde{\Theta}(1),\quad|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|,|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(\sigma_{0}),\quad|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}\big(d^{-1/2}n\big);

for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
,

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|,|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(\sigma_{0}),\quad|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|,|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(\sigma_{0}),\quad|\langle\mathbf{w}_{y_{i},r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|=\widetilde{\Theta}(1).

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is simply a combination of Lemmas
A.7
and
A.7
, where we only need to verify the bound for
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|
and
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|
. This can be done as follows:

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\leq\big[1+\Theta(\rho\eta)\big]^{T_{1}}\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{0})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\leq\exp\big[\widetilde{O}(n\rho/(d\sigma^{2}))\big]\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{0})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(\sigma_{0}),

where we use the fact that
\rho n=o(d\sigma_{p}^{2})
. The proof for
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|
will be similar and thus is ommited here.

∎

Phase 3. Training until convergence.

In this phase, we will show that the feature learning and noise learning in
Phase 2
will be maintained. Particularly, we first make the following hypothesis and then verify them via mathematical induction.

Hypothesis 1
.

For all
t=\mathrm{poly}(n)
that is greater than
T_{1}
, it holds that

(a)

We have
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
and
\sum_{r=1}^{m}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
.

(b)

We have
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}\rangle|\big)=o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)
and
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|\big)=o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)
.

(c)

We have
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\big)=o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)
and
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|\big)=o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)
.

(d)

For all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}
, we have

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|\big)=o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)
.

(e)

For all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
, we have

\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\bm{\xi})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
; for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
, we have
\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\bm{\xi})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
.

(f)

For all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
, we have

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|=o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)
; for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
, we have
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|=o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)
.

The hypothesis will be verified via induction. First, it is clear that all hypothesis are satisfied at
t=T_{1}
according to Lemma
A.8
. Then, the following lemma is useful in the entire proof.

Lemma A.9
.

Assuming all hypothesis in Hypothesis
1
hold for
\tau\in[0,t]
, then we have for all
k\in[2]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|=\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{n}{\eta}\bigg),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho n^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}\bigg),

moreover, for any
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=T_{1}}^{t}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|=\widetilde{O}(1/\eta).

Proof of Lemma
A.9
.

By (
A.2
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau+1)},\mathbf{v})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v})^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\in[n]}\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})}\alpha_{i,p}^{2}\bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v})^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|-\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\eta\alpha^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\not\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg];

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau+1)},\mathbf{u})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v})^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\in[n]}\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})}\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\cdot\alpha_{i,p}^{2}\bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v})^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}|\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}|-\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\eta\alpha^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\not\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}|\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg].

(A.16)

where we use the fact that
|\ell_{1,i}|=|\ell_{2,i}|

Summing them up and further taking a summation over
\tau\in[T_{1},t-1]
, applying Hypothesis
1
(a)
gives

\displaystyle\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|-\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\eta\alpha^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}(1).

(A.17)

where we use the fact that
|\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}|=|\ell_{2,i}^{(t)}|
and
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2},\sum_{r=1}^{m}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
.
Besides, by (
A.2
) and Hypotheses
(e)
and
(f)
, we know that the correct noise learning for different weak feature data will be different by at most

O(\mathrm{polylog}(n))
factors, therefore, we can get that

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}}\sum_{p\in[P]}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}}\sum_{p\in[P]}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}\cdot\|\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)\bigg]^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}}\sum_{p\in[P]}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\widetilde{\Theta}\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|-\widetilde{\Theta}\bigg(\frac{\eta d^{3/2}\sigma_{p}^{4}P}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg],

(A.18)

and similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}\sum_{p\in[P]}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}\sum_{p\in[P]}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\widetilde{\Theta}\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot|\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}|-\widetilde{\Theta}\bigg(\frac{\eta d^{3/2}\sigma_{p}^{4}P}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg].

Therefore, taking a summation over
r\in[m]
and
\tau\in[T_{1},t-1]
, and using the Hypothesis
1
(e)
, we have

\displaystyle\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|-\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta d^{3/2}\sigma_{p}^{4}P}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}(|{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}|)=\widetilde{O}(\rho n).

(A.19)

Combining (
A.17
) and (
A.19
) and using the fact that
d\sigma_{p}^{2}=\omega(1)
and
b\alpha^{2}=\omega(d^{3/2}\sigma_{p}^{4}P)
, we can get that

\displaystyle\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|-\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\eta\alpha^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}(\rho n).

Note that
b\alpha^{2}=o(1)
, the above inequality immediately implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho n^{2}}{\eta}\bigg).

We will further use this argument to sharpen our result. First, (
A.19
) directly leads to

\displaystyle\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}(\rho n)+\widetilde{O}\big(d^{3/2}\sigma_{p}^{4}P\rho n\big)=\widetilde{O}(\rho n),

which implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho n^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}\bigg).

Plugging the above inequality into (
A.19
) and using the fact that
b\alpha^{2}=o(1)
gives

\displaystyle\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}(1)+\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\eta\alpha^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t-1}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}(1)+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho nb\alpha^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}}\bigg)=\widetilde{O}(1).

where the last inequality is due to
\rho n=o(d\sigma_{p}^{2})
.
Further note that
|\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}|\leq 1
and
\eta=o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho n^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}\bigg)+O(\rho n)=\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho n^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}\bigg);

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{n}{\eta}\bigg)+O(n)=\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{n}{\eta}\bigg).

Moreover, by Hypothesis
1
for all
\tau\in[T_{1},t]
, we also have for all
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
,

\displaystyle|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|=\frac{\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]}{1+\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]}.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in[P]}\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle-\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in[P]}\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\pm o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg)\leq 0.

This implies that for any
i,j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
with
|\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})|=|\mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{v})|
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|}{|\ell_{1,j}^{(\tau)}|}=\Theta\bigg(\frac{\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]}{\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{j})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{j})\big]}\bigg)=\Theta\big(\exp[o\big(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)]\big)=\Theta(1).

Further note that, by Definition
3.1
, the number of feature patches are uniformly sampled from
[1,\Theta(1)]
, implying that with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, for any
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\#\big\{j:j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+},|\mathcal{P}_{j}(\mathbf{v})|=|\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})|\big\}=\Theta(n)

Therefore, let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
be the above set of data points, we have for any
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
or
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}|\ell_{1,s}^{(\tau)}|

\displaystyle=\Theta\big(|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}|^{-1}\big)\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\Theta\big(|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}|^{-1}\big)\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg).

where the last inequality is due to
|{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}|=\Theta(n)
. This completes the proof.
∎

We will then verify Hypothesis
1
(c)
, which is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma A.10
.

Let Hypothesis
1
holds for all
\tau\leq t
, then we have

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\big)
and
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|\big)
.

Proof of Lemma
A.10
.

Recall the update of rare features in (
A.2
), we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau+1)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}}\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})}\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau+1)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}}\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})}\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle.

Then according to the Hypothesis
1
(c)
for all
\tau\in[T_{1},t]
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\big)+\frac{\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|\cdot O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\big),

where the last inequality is due to the fact that
|\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})|=\Theta(1)
. By Lemma
A.9
, it is clear that
\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|=\widetilde{O}\big(\frac{\rho n^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}\big)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\leq O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\big)+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho n}{d\sigma^{2}}\bigg)\cdot O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\big)=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle|\big).

The proof for
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle|
is similar so we omit it here.
∎

Using the similar proof technique, we are able to verify Hypothesis
1
(b)
,
1
(d)
, and
1
(f)
, which are summarized in the following lemmas.

Lemma A.11
.

Let Hypothesis
1
holds for all
\tau\leq t
, then we have

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|\big)
and
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{u}\rangle|\big)
.

Proof of Lemma
A.11
.

Since the proofs for
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|
and
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle|
are basically identical, we will only provide the proof regarding
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|
. By (
A.2
) and data distribution in Definition
3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})}\alpha_{i,p}^{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\sum_{i\in[n]\backslash{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\mathbf{v})}\alpha_{i,p}^{2}\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle+\frac{2\eta b\alpha^{2}}{n}\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}|.

Taking an absolute value on both sides and then applying Hypothesis
1
(b)
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|+\frac{2\eta b\alpha^{2}}{n}\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|+O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|\big)\cdot O\bigg(\frac{\eta b\alpha^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\rho n^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}+\frac{n}{\eta}\bigg)

\displaystyle=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\mathbf{v}\rangle|\big),

where the second inequality is by Lemma
A.9
and the last inequality is due to the fact that
\rho n=o(d\sigma_{p}^{2})
and
b\alpha^{2}=o(1)
.
This completes the proof.

∎

Lemma A.12
.

Let Hypothesis
1
holds for all
\tau\leq t
, then we have

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|=o\big(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}
,
r\in[m]
,
k\in[2]
, and
q\in[P]
.

Proof of Lemma
A.12
.

By (
A.2
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot|\ell_{k,s}^{(t)}|+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{p\in[P]}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{k,i}^{(t)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot|\ell_{k,s}^{(t)}|

\displaystyle\qquad+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{p\in[P]}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|.

Then by Hypotheses
1
, we can further get

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg)\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}|\ell_{k,s}^{(t)}|+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|.

(A.20)

Note that
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}
,
then by Lemma
A.9
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=T_{1}}^{t}|\ell_{k,s}^{(\tau)}|=\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg),\quad\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{n}{\eta}\bigg).

Therefore, plugging the above inequalities into (
A.20
) gives

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}

\displaystyle\rangle|\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg)\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg)+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{n}{\eta}\bigg)

\displaystyle=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg)+o\bigg(\frac{d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)+\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg),

where we use the fact that
d\sigma_{p}^{2}=o(n)
and
d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}=o(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n))
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.13
.

Let Hypothesis
1
holds for all
\tau\leq t
, then we have

|\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|=O\big(|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\big)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
,
r\in[m]
,
k\neq y_{s}
, and
q\in[P]
.

Proof of Lemma
A.13
.

Similar to the previous proof, we will only prove the argument for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
, the proof for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
can be performed using exactly the same analysis. By (
A.2
), we have for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|-|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|\cdot\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot|\ell_{2,s}^{(t)}|+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{p\in[P]}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{2,i}^{(t)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(P)}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|

\displaystyle=|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(T_{1})},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle|+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{2,i}^{(\tau)}|

\displaystyle=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg),

where the last inequality is by Lemma
A.9
.
This completes the proof.

∎

Finally, we will verify the common features learning (Hypothesis
1
(a)
) and noise learning for rare feature data (Hypothesis
1
(e)
).

Lemma A.14
.

Let Hypothesis
1
holds for all
\tau\leq t
, then we have
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
and
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
.

Proof of Lemma
A.14
.

We first prove the upper bound:
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\leq\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
. Particularly, by (
A.2
), (
A.9
) and Definition
3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}|+\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\big)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}|\bigg].

Therefore, we can get that

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}}|\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}|+\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}|\bigg],

(A.21)

where we use the fact that
|\ell_{1,i}^{(t)}|=|\ell_{2,i}^{(t)}|
. By Hypothesis
1
, we have for all
\tau\leq t
and
i
,

\displaystyle\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}=\frac{\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})}{1+\exp\big[F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})-F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(\tau)};\mathbf{x}_{i})\big]}=\exp\bigg[-\Theta\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}\bigg)\bigg].

Therefore, let
a_{\tau}:=\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}
, we have the following according to (
A.21
)

\displaystyle a_{\tau+1}\leq a_{\tau}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta(\eta)\cdot e^{-ca_{\tau}}+\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg],

(A.22)

where
c
is an absolute positive constant. Let
T=\mathrm{polylog}(n)
be the total iteration number, then we will show that
a_{t}\leq 3c^{-1}\log(T)
for all
\tau\leq t
. Particularly, we will prove that either (1)
a_{\tau}<2c^{-1}\log(T)
or (2)
a_{\tau}>2\log(T)>a_{\tau-1}
but it will not reach
3\log(T)
as
\tau
increases before it becomes less than
2c^{-1}\log(T)
again. The first case immediately implies that
a_{\tau}<3c^{-1}\log(T)
, so we will only need to focus on case (2). In this case, we have
a_{\tau}\leq a_{\tau-1}+\Theta(\eta)\leq 2.1c^{-1}\log(T)
. Then before
a_{\tau}
becomes less than
2c^{-1}\log(T)
, we have for any
\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau,t]
that

\displaystyle a_{\tau^{\prime}}\leq a_{\tau}+\sum_{s=\tau}^{\tau^{\prime}-1}a_{s}\cdot\bigg[\Theta(\eta)\cdot e^{-ca_{s}}+\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(s)}|\bigg].

Note that
a_{s}\cdot e^{-ca_{s}}\leq 2c^{-1}\log(T)/T^{2}\leq 0.1c^{-1}/T
if
T=\omega(1)
, then using the fact that
\eta=o(1)
,

\displaystyle a_{\tau^{\prime}}\leq a_{\tau}+\sum_{s=\tau}^{\tau^{\prime}-1}\bigg[\frac{\eta}{T}+\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot a_{s}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(s)}|\bigg]\leq 2.2c^{-1}\log(T)+\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{s=\tau}^{\tau^{\prime}-1}a_{s}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(s)}|.

Then as long as
a_{s}<10c^{-1}\log(T)
for
s\in[\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
, we have the following according to Lemma
A.9
,

\displaystyle\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{s=\tau}^{\tau^{\prime}-1}a_{s}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(s)}|=\widetilde{O}\bigg(b\alpha^{2}+\frac{\rho nb\alpha^{2}}{d\sigma_{p}^{2}}\bigg)=o(1)\leq 0.1c^{-1}\log(T),

where we use the fact that
b\alpha^{2}=o(1)
and
d\sigma_{p}^{2}=\omega(\rho n)
. Therefore, we can conclude that before
\alpha_{\tau}^{\prime}
reaches
10c^{-1}\log(T)
, it must satisfy

\displaystyle a_{\tau^{\prime}}\leq 2.3c^{-1}\log(T),

for any
\tau^{\prime}\leq t
. This further implies that

\displaystyle a_{t+1}\leq a_{t}+\widetilde{O}(\eta)\leq 3c^{-1}\log(T)=O\big(\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big),

which completes the proof of
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{O}(1)
.

The next step is to show that
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{v}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}(1)
. Similar to (
A.22
), we can get that

\displaystyle a_{\tau+1}\geq a_{\tau}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta(\eta)\cdot e^{-Ca_{\tau}}-\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg],

where
C
is an absolute positive constant. In fact, we must have
a_{\tau}\geq\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}
since otherwise,

\displaystyle a_{\tau+1}\geq a_{\tau}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta(\eta)\cdot e^{-Ca_{\tau}}-\Theta\bigg(\frac{b\alpha^{2}\eta}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg]\geq a_{\tau}\cdot\big[1+\Theta(\eta)\big],

where the first inequality is due to
e^{-ca_{\tau}}=\Theta(1)
if
a_{\tau}=O(1)
and the second inequality is due to
|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\leq 1
and
b\alpha^{2}=o(1)
. This implies that
a_{\tau+1}
will keep increase, which will at least continue to the case that
a_{\tau}>1
. This completes the proof that
a_{t+1}=\widetilde{\Omega}(1)
.

The proof for
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
will be basically the same so we omit it here.

∎

Lemma A.15
.

Let Hypothesis
1
holds for all
\tau\leq t
, then we have
\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{s}(\bm{\xi})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
, and
\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{s}(\bm{\xi})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
.

Proof of Lemma
A.15
.

Note that
P,m=\Theta(\mathrm{polylog}(n))
, it suffices to prove that
\max_{q,r}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}

and
\max_{q,r}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
. In the following proof we will only consider
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
as the proof for
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
will exactly the same.

By (
A.2
), we have for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle\cdot\bigg[1+\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\ell_{1,s}^{(\tau)}\cdot\|\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg]\pm\frac{2\eta}{n}\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(d^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}\big)\cdot\sum_{i\neq s||p\neq q}|\ell_{k,i}^{(\tau)}|\cdot|\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle|.

(A.23)

We first prove the upper bound of
\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{s}(\bm{\xi})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}
. Then, using the Hypothesis
1

(e)
, we have for any
i\in[n]
,
s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
,
r\in[m]
, and
p\in[P]

\displaystyle(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}\leq O\bigg(\mathrm{polylog}(n)\bigg)\cdot O(mP)\cdot\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}=O\bigg(\mathrm{polylog}(n)\bigg)\cdot\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}.

Then (
A.23
) implies that

\displaystyle\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\ell_{1,s}^{(\tau)}+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg].

Then by Hypothesis
1
, we can further get that the quantity
\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{s}(\bm{\xi})}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}
will be the dominating term in the neural network output function, so that
\ell_{1,s}^{(\tau)}\geq e^{-c\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}}
for some constant
c
. Therefore, let
a_{\tau}=\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}
, we can follow the similar derivation of (
A.22
). Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle a_{\tau+1}\leq a_{\tau}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot e^{-ca_{\tau}}+\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg]

Then we can follow the exact proof technique in Lemma
A.14
to conclude that
a_{t+1}=\widetilde{O}(1)
, while it only requires to verify that

\displaystyle\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\tau=T_{1}}^{t}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|=o(1),

which clearly holds by Lemma
A.9
and the fact that
Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}=o(1)
.

The lower bound can be similarly obtained as the following can be deduced by (
A.23
):

\displaystyle\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau+1)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{r,q}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(\tau)},\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\ell_{1,s}^{(\tau)}-\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg],

which leads to

\displaystyle a_{\tau+1}\geq a_{\tau}\cdot\bigg[1+\Theta\bigg(\frac{\eta d\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot e^{-Ca_{\tau}}-\widetilde{O}\bigg(\frac{\eta Pd^{1/2}\sigma_{p}^{2}}{n}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{1,i}^{(\tau)}|\bigg]

for some absolute constant
C
. Then following the same proof of Lemma
A.14
, we can get that
a_{t+1}=\widetilde{\Omega}(1)
. This completes the proof.
∎

A.3
Proof of Theorem
4.1

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We first show that
\|\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(T)}\|_{2}=\widetilde{O}(n)
for all
k\in[2]
and
r\in[m]
. In particular, note that the update of standard training is always the linear combination of all critical vectors, i.e.,
\mathbf{v}
,
\mathbf{u}
,
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}
’s. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)}=\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(0)}+\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\mathbf{v}+\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathbf{u}+\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}+\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{u}^{\prime}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\bm{\xi})}\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)})\cdot\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}.

Here we use
\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{a})
to denote the coefficient of
\mathbf{a}
for all
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}
. Then by Lemma
5.3
and using the fact that
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\|_{2},\|\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\|_{2}=1
, we have

\displaystyle|\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v})|,|\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{u})|=\widetilde{O}(1),\quad|\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})|,|\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})|=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg).

Moreover, using the fact that
|\langle\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)},\bm{\xi}_{j}^{(q)}\rangle|=o(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n))
for any
i\neq j
or
p\neq q
, applying Lemma
5.3
and the fact that
\|\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\|_{2}^{2}=\Omega(1)
for all
i\in[n]
and
p\in[P]
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i}(\bm{\xi})}\rho_{k,r}^{(t)}(\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)})\cdot\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\bigg\|_{2}^{2}\leq\widetilde{O}(n^{2}).

Combining the above results, we can readily conclude that
\|\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)}\|_{2}=\widetilde{O}(n)
.

Then we will characterize the test errors for common
feature data and rare feature data separately.
Regarding the common feature data, we can take a positive common feature data
(\mathbf{x},1)
as an example and obtain the following by Lemma
5.3
,

\displaystyle F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})=\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p=1}^{P}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\rangle\big)^{2}\geq\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1).

(A.24)

Besides, we have the following regarding
F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})
:

\displaystyle F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\big)^{2}+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\neq\mathbf{v}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\rangle\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\neq\mathbf{v}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\rangle\big)^{2}+o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg).

(A.25)

where we use the result
|\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle|=o\big(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)
. Then, note that if
\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\neq\mathbf{v}
, it can be either feature noise (i.e.,
\alpha\mathbf{u}
or
\alpha\mathbf{v}
) or random noise
\bm{\zeta}_{i}^{(p)}
, which is independent of the random noise vectors in the training data points (i.e.,
\{\bm{\xi}\}
). Therefore, using the result that
\|\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)}\|_{2}=\widetilde{O}(n)
, we can obtain with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Omega(d^{1/2}))
, it holds that for all
r\in[m]

\displaystyle(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\bm{\zeta}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{O}(\sigma_{p}^{2}n^{2}).

(A.26)

Besides, note that there are at most
b
patches within the total
P
patches that are feature noise, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\neq\mathbf{v}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\rangle\big)^{2}\leq O(mb\alpha^{2})+\widetilde{O}(mP\sigma_{p}^{2}n^{2})=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg),

where the last equality is by the data model in Definition
3.1
:
b\alpha^{2}=o\big(1/\mathrm{polylog}(n)\big)
and
\sigma_{p}=o(d^{-1/2}n^{1/2})
.
Therefore, comparing (
A.24
) and (
A.3
), we can get
F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})>F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})
with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
.

Then we will move on to study the rare feature data. In particular, we consider the rare feature data with incorrect feature noise. Without loss of generality, we take a positive data
(\mathbf{x},1)
as an example, which contains rare feature
\mathbf{v}
and incorrect feature noise
\alpha\mathbf{u}
. Then we can get the following results for
F_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle F_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle\big)^{2}+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{u}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\alpha\mathbf{u}\rangle\big)^{2}+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\not\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\rangle\big)^{2}.

Note that if
\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\not\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
, then
\mathbf{x}^{(p)}
must be a random noise vector that is independent of
\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)}
. To begin with, the first two terms of the above equation for different
k
’s can be bounded by applying Lemma
5.3
(particularly
\sum_{r=1}^{m}(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle)^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}(1)
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{O}(\sigma_{0}^{2}),\quad\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{u}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{1,r}^{(t)},\alpha\mathbf{u}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{O}(b\alpha^{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}),

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{O}(\sigma_{0}^{2}),\quad\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{u}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{2,r}^{(t)},\alpha\mathbf{u}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}(\alpha^{2}).

Moreover, by (
A.26
), we can further get that with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Omega(d^{1/2}))>1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r=1}^{m}\sum_{p:\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\not\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}}\big(\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}^{(p)}\rangle\big)^{2}=\widetilde{O}(mP\sigma_{p}^{2}n^{2})=o(\alpha^{2}).

where the last equality is by our data model in Definition
3.1
. This further implies that
conditioning on
\mathbf{W}^{(t)}
, with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, we have

\displaystyle F_{2}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})>F_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})

on the positive rare feature data that has incorrect feature noise.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{rare}}}[\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{k}F_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})\neq y]\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}\geq\frac{1}{2.01}.

Therefore, combining the test error analysis for common feature data and rare feature data and using the fact that the fraction of rare feature data is
\rho
, we can finally obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim\mathcal{D}}[\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{k}F_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})\neq y]\geq\rho\cdot\mathbb{P}_{(\mathbf{x},y)\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{rare}}}[\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{k}F_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x})\neq y]\geq\frac{\rho}{2.01}.

This completes the proof.

∎

Appendix B
Mixup data

B.1
Characterization of the mixup dataset

Category of different Mixup data patches.

First recall the category of different Mixup training data points:

•

Mix between two common feature data points, including
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,0}^{+,+}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,0}^{-,-}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,0}^{+,-}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,0}^{-,+}
, each of them is of size
\Theta(n^{2})
.

•

Mix between common feature and rare feature data points with the same label, including
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,1}^{+,+}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,1}^{-,-}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,0}^{+,+}
, and
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,0}^{-,-}
, each of them is of size
\Theta(\rho n^{2})
.

•

Mix between common feature and rare feature data points with different labels, including
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,1}^{+,-}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{0,1}^{-,+}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,0}^{+,-}
, and
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,0}^{-,+}
, each of them is of size
\Theta(\rho n^{2})
.

•

Mix between two rare feature data points, including
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,1}^{+,+}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,1}^{-,-}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,1}^{+,-}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{1,1}^{-,+}
, each of them is of size
\Theta(\rho^{2}n^{2})
.

Then, given
n^{2}
mixed data points, we have in total
n^{2}P
data patches. Besides, note that in the original dataset that consists of
n
training data points, each data patch
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}\in\big\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\alpha\mathbf{u},\alpha\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\big\}.

Moreover, by the data distribution defined in Definition
3.1
, we have

•

\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{u}
will appear in
\Theta(n)
data and
\Theta(n)
data patches.

•

\alpha\mathbf{v}
and
\alpha\mathbf{u}
will appear in
n
data and
\Theta(bn)
data patches.

•

\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
will appear in
\Theta(\rho n)
data and
\Theta(\rho n)
data patches.

•

\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}
, if it is not zero, will appear in one data and one data patch.

Then based on the above facts, we provide the following lemma that characterizes the number of different types of data patches on the mixup dataset.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}:=\{\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}\}_{i,j\in[n],p\in[P]}
be the collection of all data patches of the mixup dataset, then among these
n^{2}P
data patches, with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, let
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{a}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
, we have

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}\}

will appear in
\Theta(n^{2}/P)
data patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}
will appear in
\Theta(\rho n^{2}/P)
patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
will appear in
O(bn^{2}/P)
patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}
will appear in
\Theta(n^{2})
patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\alpha\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}
will appear in
\Theta(\rho^{2}n^{2}/P)
data patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\alpha\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
will appear in
O(\rho bn^{2}/P)
patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\alpha\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}
will appear in
\Theta(\rho n^{2})
patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
will appear in
O(b^{2}n^{2}/P)
patches.

•

The vector with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}
will appear in
O(bn^{2})
patches.

Besides, regarding any non-zero noise vector
\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}
, we have, among the collection of data patches
\{\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}\}_{j\in[n]}
, with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
,

•

\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\bm{\xi}_{i,j}^{(p)}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}\}
will appear in
\Theta(n/P)
patches.

•

\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\bm{\xi}_{i,j}^{(p)}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\bm{b}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
will appear in
O(bn/P)
patches.

•

\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\bm{\xi}_{i,j}^{(p)}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}
will appear in
\Theta(\rho n/P)
patches.

•

\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\bm{\xi}_{i,j}^{(p)}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\bm{b}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}
will appear in
\Theta(n)
patches.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

We first consider a fixed
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and the corresponding collection of data patches
\{\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}\}_{j\in[n],p\in[P]}
. Then by Definition
3.1
, conditioning on
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}
, we have for any
j\neq i

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]=\Theta\bigg(\frac{1}{P}\bigg).

Therefore, we can further get that conditioning on
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}
, the summation
\sum_{j\neq i}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]
follows Binomial distribution
\mathrm{Binom}(n-1,p)
with probability parameter
p=\Theta(1/P)
. Then by Hoeffding’s inequality, we can get that with probability at least
1-\exp(-n^{2}/P^{2})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[n]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]=\Theta\bigg(\frac{n}{P}\bigg).

Note that we have at least
\Theta(n)
number of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
’s that consist of the common feature vector
\mathbf{v}
, then applying union bound over these
\mathbf{x}_{i}
’s, we can further get with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\sum_{p\in[P]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p_{i})}=\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p_{i})}=\mathbf{v}]\cdot\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p_{i})}=\mathbf{v}]

\displaystyle\geq\Theta(n)\cdot\Theta\bigg(\frac{n}{P}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\Theta\bigg(\frac{n^{2}}{P}\bigg).

Here we define
p_{i}
as the index of the data patch that is
\mathbf{v}
if the data
\mathbf{x}_{i}
has such a common feature vector, otherwise,
p_{i}
is arbitrarily chosen.
On the other hand, we can also get

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\sum_{p\in[P]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]\leq\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\sum_{p\in[P]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]\cdot\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]\leq n\cdot\Theta(1)\cdot\Theta\bigg(\frac{n}{P}\bigg)=\Theta\bigg(\frac{n^{2}}{P}\bigg),

where the second inequality is due to that each data will have at most
\Theta(1)
patches being
\mathbf{v}
. Similarly, we can also prove the same results for the case of
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{a}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\mathbf{a},\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}
.

The proof for the case of
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{a}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
will be also similar, the only difference is that conditioning on
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}
, the probability of
\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{v}

or
\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{u}
will be
O(b/P)
. Finally, we can get that (here we take
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{v}
and
\bm{b}=\mathbf{v}
as an example)

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\sum_{p\in[P]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{v}+\alpha(1-\lambda)\mathbf{v}]=\Theta(n)\cdot\Theta\bigg(\frac{bn}{P}\bigg)=\Theta\bigg(\frac{bn^{2}}{P}\bigg).

The proof for the case of
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{a}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\}
will also be similar, where we only need to use the fact that
\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]=\Theta(\rho/P)
. Here we take
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{v}
and
\bm{b}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
as an example.

Regarding the case of
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{a}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}
, we only need to use the fact that
\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{v}+(1-\lambda)\bm{\xi}_{j}^{(p)}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]=\Theta(1)
, where we take
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{v}
as an example. Then the desired result can be proved in a similar way.

When
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}
we will also need to use the fact that we have in total
\Theta(\rho n)
number of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
’s that consist of
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
or
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
. Then take
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
and
\bm{b}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
as an example, conditioning on
\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
, we have for any
j\neq i

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=\Theta\bigg(\frac{\rho}{P}\bigg).

Therefore, we can get that with probability at least
1-1/\mathrm{poly}(n)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[n]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=\Theta\bigg(\frac{\rho n}{P}\bigg).

Accordingly, we can further obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\sum_{p\in[P]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\sum_{p\in[P]}\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]\cdot\ind[\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=\Theta(\rho n)\cdot\Theta\bigg(\frac{\rho n}{P}\bigg)=\Theta\bigg(\frac{\rho^{2}n^{2}}{P}\bigg).

The proof for the case of
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\lambda\mathbf{a}+(1-\lambda)\bm{b}
with
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\}
and
\bm{b}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{v}\}
or
\bm{b}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}
will also be similar, where we only need to use the fact that
\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=O(b/P)
and
\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\bm{\xi}_{j}^{(p)}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=\Theta(1)
.

When
\mathbf{a}\in\{\alpha\mathbf{v},\alpha\mathbf{u}\}
we only need to use the fact that we have in total
\Theta(n)
number of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
’s that consist of
\Theta(b)
number of
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}
or
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
. The remaining proof will be similar to previous ones based on the fact that
\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{v}]=O(b/P)
and
\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\bm{\xi}_{j}^{(p)}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{v}]=\Theta(1)
, where we take
\mathbf{a}=\alpha\mathbf{v}
and
\bm{b}=\alpha\mathbf{v}
as an example.

Lastly, we will move on to the case of
\mathbf{a}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}
. In this case, we only need to use the facts that for any
j\neq i
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}]=\Theta(1/P)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{u}]=\Theta(1/P)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{v}^{\prime}]=\Theta(\rho/P)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}]=\Theta(\rho/P)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{v}]=O(b/P)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{u}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}=\alpha\mathbf{u}]=O(b/P)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{(p)}=\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[\mathbf{x}_{j}^{(p)}\in\{\bm{\xi}\}]=\Theta(1).

Then applying the standard concentration argument for binomial distribution yields the desired results.

∎

B.2
Learning Dynamics of Feature and Noise vectors

Now, we will seek to study the learning of feature and noise vectors. Particularly, the update formulas of all feature vectors are provided as follows: for any
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{a}\rangle-\eta\cdot\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{k,r}}L(\mathbf{W}^{(t)}),\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{a}\rangle+\frac{\eta}{n^{2}}\cdot\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\ell_{k,(i,j)}^{(t)}\sum_{p\in[P]}\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}\rangle\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{a}\rangle

(B.1)

More specifically, we summarize the update of all critical vectors (e.g., common features, rare features, and data noise vectors) in the following Proposition.

Proposition B.2
.

For any critical vector
\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}
, we have

\displaystyle-\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{k,r}}L_{{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)}),\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{a})\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}\rangle+\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{a})\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}\rangle+\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{a})\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{a})\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{p\in[P]}\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)},\mathbf{a})\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{\xi}_{i}^{(p)}\rangle,

where
\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\bm{b},\mathbf{a})
is a scalar output function that depends on
\bm{b},\mathbf{a}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}
. More specifically, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}=\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\mathbf{v}+\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{u})\cdot\mathbf{u}+\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{v}^{\prime}+\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{u}^{\prime}+\sum_{s=1}^{n}\sum_{q\in[P]}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)})\cdot\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)}

(B.2)

be a linear expansion of
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}
on the space spanned by
\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\bm{b},\mathbf{a})=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\ell_{k,(i,j)}^{(t)}\sum_{p\in[P]}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\bm{b})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{a}\rangle.

(B.3)

Proof of Proposition
B.2
.

Recall (
B.2
) and the decomposition of
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}
in (
B.2
), we have

\displaystyle-\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{k,r}}L_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)}),\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\ell_{k,(i,j)}^{(t)}\sum_{p\in[P]}\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}\rangle\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\ell_{k,(i,j)}^{(t)}\sum_{p\in[P]}\sum_{\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\bm{b})\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{b}\rangle\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{a}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}}\bigg[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\ell_{k,(i,j)}^{(t)}\sum_{p\in[P]}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\bm{b})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{a}\rangle\bigg]\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{b}\rangle.

Therefore, it is easy to see that using the definition of
\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\bm{b},\mathbf{a})
in (
B.3
), we have

\displaystyle-\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{w}_{k,r}}L_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)}),\mathbf{a}\rangle=\sum_{\bm{b}\in\{\mathbf{v},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\}\cup\{\bm{\xi}\}}\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\bm{b},\mathbf{a})\cdot\langle\mathbf{w}_{k,r}^{(t)},\bm{b}\rangle,

which completes the proof.

∎

Note that the neural network outputs are in the order of
o(1)
in the first few iterations, which implies that the output logits are within the range
[0.5-o(1),0.5+o(1)]
. Further note that the loss derivatives
\ell_{k;(i,j)}^{(t)}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\ell_{k;(i,j)}^{(t)}|\in\big\{1-\mathrm{Logit}_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i,j}),\mathrm{Logit}_{k}(\mathbf{w}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i,j}),\lambda-\mathrm{Logit}_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i,j}),\mathrm{Logit}_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i,j})+\lambda-1\big\},

which will also be in the constant order. Then similar to the previous analysis on the standard training, we will directly take
|\ell_{k,(i,j)}^{(t)}|=\Theta(1)
when characterizing the learning of feature and noise vectors in the initial phase.

Then, the challenging part in the analysis is the characterization of the mixed data patches
\{\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)}\}_{p\in[P]}
, since it can be: mixture of common features, mixture of rare features, mixture of common and rare features, mixture of feature and noise, which will produce different gradients. For any mixed data
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}=\lambda\mathbf{x}_{i}+(1-\lambda)\mathbf{x}_{j}
, we will denote it as the positive mixed data if
y_{i}=1
and the negative mixed data if
y_{i}=-1
. The following lemma gives the characterization of the data patch of all mixed data.

B.3
Characterizing the Coefficient
\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\cdot,\cdot)

B.3.1
Correct Common Feature Learning

Lemma B.3
.

Assume
\max_{k\in[2],(i,j)\in{\mathcal{S}}}|F_{k}(\mathbf{W}^{(t)};\mathbf{x}_{i,j})|\leq\zeta\in\big[\omega(b\alpha),o\big(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\big)\big]
, then recalling the update form in Proposition
B.2
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})=\Theta(1),\quad|\gamma_{1}^{(t)}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})|=O(\zeta+\alpha),\quad|\gamma_{1}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v}^{\prime},\mathbf{v})|=O(\rho/P),

\displaystyle|\gamma_{1}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime},\mathbf{v})|=O(\zeta\rho/P),\quad|\gamma_{1}^{(t)}(\bm{\xi}_{s}^{(q)},\mathbf{v})|=\widetilde{O}\big(1/(Pn)\big).

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

We will prove all the arguments in order.

Proof for
\gamma_{1}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})
.

We first prove the bound for
\gamma_{1}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})
. By (
B.3
), we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}^{(t)}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v})=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[n]}\ell_{k,(i,j)}^{(t)}\sum_{p\in[P]}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle,

(B.4)

where
\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})=\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle
. Therefore, we only need to consider the data patches that contain
\mathbf{v}
(including common feature
\mathbf{v}
and feature noise
\alpha\mathbf{v}
). The regarding the mixed data
\mathbf{x}_{i,j}
, we consider the following cases

•

i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
;

•

i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
, and
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
;

•

i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
, and
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}

•

i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{-}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{-}
.

Analysis on the data
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
In particular, note that before the mixup, both the data
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{x}_{j}
have a constant number of common feature patches. Therefore, let
\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})
denote the set of patches with the common feature
\mathbf{v}
(which appears in either
\mathbf{x}_{i}
or
\mathbf{x}_{j}
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in[P]}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle+\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}(\mathbf{v})\backslash\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle.

(B.5)

Regarding the first term on the R.H.S. of the above equation, by Definition
3.1
, we know that there exists at least one common feature patch in both
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{x}_{j}
, which leads to
\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}\geq\lambda
for at least one
p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(vb)
. This further gives

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})}[\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})]^{2}\geq\lambda^{2}.

Besides, we also have that the number of common feature patches are upper bounded by some constant (i.e.,
|\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})|=\Theta(1)
), this further leads to

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle\leq\Theta(1).

Regarding the second term on the R.H.S. of (
B.5
), we have
\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}\leq\alpha
since
\mathbf{v}
can only appear in the form of feature noise. Besides, by Definition
3.1
, we know that the number of patches containing feature noise is at most
b
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}(\mathbf{v})\backslash\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{i,j}(\mathbf{v})\backslash\mathcal{P}_{i,j}^{*}(\mathbf{v})}[\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})]^{2}\leq b\alpha^{2}=o\bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(n)}\bigg).

Moreover, note that in the initial phase we have
\ell_{1,(i,j)}^{(t)}=\Theta(1)
for
(i,j)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0,0}^{+,+}
, we can further get that

\displaystyle\ell_{1,(i,j)}^{(t)}\sum_{p\in[P]}\theta_{i,j}^{(p)}(\mathbf{v})\cdot\langle\mathbf{x}_{i,j}^{(p)},\mathbf{v}\rangle=\Theta(1).

Analysis on the data
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
and
j\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{+}
.

The analysis for this type of data will be similar. In fact, we will consider two types of data:
i\in{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{+}
and
